, so that
\sup_{t\leq T}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\leq r_{K}
. This proves the first claim of the lemma.

By Markov’s Inequality, and recalling that
r_{k}
is w.p. 1 non-decreasing and

\sup_{t\leq T}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\leq R_{K}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}^{2}\geq 2^{-i/2-4}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{r_{K}^{2}}\right]\cdot 2^{i/2+4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{{(40\delta^{i}{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{2}+2L_{R}d+L_{\beta}^{\prime})}T}T^{2}\cdot{({\delta^{i}}^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-i/2+14}

(A.12)

This proves the second claim of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5
.

Let
x\in M
be some initial point and
E
an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}M
. Let
\mathbf{B}(t)
be a Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; and
\beta(x)
a vector field satisfying Assumption
3
. Let
T\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, for
t\in[0,T]
and let
x^{i}(t)
be constructed as per (
4.2
).
Then with probability 1, there is a limit
x(t)
such that for all
\varepsilon
, there exists an integer
N
such that for all
i\geq N
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x(t))}\leq\varepsilon.

Proof of Lemma
5
.

Let us define
L_{0}:={\left\|\beta(x(0))\right\|}
.

Step 1: Bounding the probability of deviation between
x^{i}
and
x^{i+1}

We would like to apply Lemma
4
. However, note that Lemma
4
assumes that
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}
globally, which we do not assume here. We must therefore approximate
\beta
by a sequence of Lipschitz vector fields.

Let us define

\displaystyle\beta^{j}(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\beta(x)&\text{for }{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq 2^{j/2}\\
\beta(x)\cdot\frac{2^{j/2}}{{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}}&\text{for }{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}>2^{j/2}\end{array}\right.

Let us denote by
L_{\beta^{j}}:=2^{j/2}
. We verify that for all
x,y\in M
,
{\left\|\beta^{j}(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta^{j}}
and

{\left\|\beta^{j}(x)-\Gamma_{y}^{x}\beta^{j}(y)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}^{\prime}{\left\|x-y\right\|}
.

Finally, for any let
\tilde{x}^{i,j}(t)
be as defined in (
A.3
), with
\beta
replaced by
\beta^{j}
. Lemma
4
immediately implies that, for all
i\geq{\mathcal{C}}
(where
{\mathcal{C}}
is some constant depending on
L_{R},T,d
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}-2}})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{{(40T{L_{R}}+2L_{R}d+L_{\beta}^{\prime})}T}T^{2}\cdot{(T^{3}L_{R}^{2}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+T{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-i/2+14}

where we use the fact that
\delta^{i}{L_{\beta}^{i}}^{2}=T
by definition.

Recalling that
\beta^{j}(x)=\beta(x)
unless
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\geq 2^{j/2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{t\in[0,T]}x^{i}(t)\neq\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t)})}=\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}x^{i}_{k}\neq\tilde{x}^{i,i}_{k}})}\leq\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}{\left\|\beta(x^{i}_{k})\right\|}\geq 2^{i/2}})}

We can bound
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{0}+L_{\beta}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{(x,x_{0})}
, so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}{\left\|\beta(x^{i}_{k})\right\|}\geq 2^{i/2}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k})}\geq\frac{2^{i/2}-L_{0}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp{(2+8TL_{\beta}^{\prime}+TL_{R}d+TL_{R}L_{0}^{2})}\cdot{(2Td+4T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}\cdot{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\cdot 2^{-i+2}

where we use Lemma
15
, with
K=2^{i}
, and assume that
i
satisfies
2^{i/2}\geq L_{0}
and
2^{i}\geq T
.

Using identical steps, we can also bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{t\in[0,T]}x^{i+1}(t)\neq\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t)})}\leq\exp{(2+8TL_{\beta}^{\prime}+TL_{R}d+TL_{R}L_{0}^{2})}\cdot{(2Td+4T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}\cdot{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\cdot 2^{-i+2}

Put together,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{({x}^{i}(t),{x}^{i+1}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}-2}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}-2}})}+\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{t\in[0,T]}x^{i}(t)\neq\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t)})}+\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{t\in[0,T]}x^{i+1}(t)\neq\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\cdot 2^{-i/2}

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
is a constant that depends on
T,L_{R},L_{\beta}^{\prime},L_{0},d
, but does not depend on
i
.

Step 2: Apply Borel-Cantelli to show uniformly-Cauchy sequence with probability 1

Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{i={\mathcal{C}}_{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}}})}<\infty

By the Borel-Cantelli Lemma,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}}\text{ for infinitely many $i$}})}=0

Equivalently, with probability
1
, for all
\varepsilon
, there exists a
N
such that for all
i\geq N
,

\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\leq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}}
. For any
j\geq i\geq N
, it then follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{j}(t))}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=i}^{j}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x^{\ell+1}(t))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=i}^{j}2^{-\frac{\ell}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6\cdot 2^{-i/4}

Step 3: Uniform-Cauchy sequence implies uniform convergence to limit using standard arguments

Therefore, with probability 1,
x^{i}(t)
is a uniformly Cauchy sequence. Let
x(t)
be the point-wise limit of
x^{i}(t)
, as
i\to\infty
. It follows
4
4

4

A nice clean proof can be seen at
https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/1287669/uniformly-cauchy-sequences
that with probability 1, for any
\varepsilon
, there exists a
N
such that for all
i\geq N
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x(t))}\leq\varepsilon

∎

Lemma 6
.

Let
\beta(\cdot)
be a vector field satisfying Assumption
3
. Assume also that there exists
L_{\beta}
such that
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}
for all
x
. Consider arbitrary
x_{0}\in M
and let
E
be an orthonormal basis of
T_{x_{0}}M
. Let
\mathbf{B}
be a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
x^{i}(t)=\overline{\Phi}(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B},i)
and
x(t)=\Phi(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B})
as defined in (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) respectively. (Existence of
x(t)
follows from Lemma
5
).

Then for any non-negative integer
\ell
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x(t))}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle 2^{14}e^{40T\delta^{\ell}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2TL_{R}d+TL_{\beta}^{\prime}}T^{3}{({\delta^{\ell}}^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{\ell}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-\ell}

where
\delta^{i}:=2^{-i}T

Proof.

Consider any fixed
i
, let
\delta^{i}:=T/2^{i}
and let
K:=T/\delta^{i}=2^{i}
as in (
4.1
).

By the first claim of Lemma
4
, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{10}\cdot e^{40K{\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2K\delta^{i}L_{R}d+K\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime}}{(K{\delta^{i}})}^{2}{({\delta^{i}}^{4}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+{\delta^{i}}L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{2^{10}e^{40T\delta^{i}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2TL_{R}d+TL_{\beta}^{\prime}}T^{3}{({\delta^{i}}^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}}_{:=s_{i}}\cdot 2^{-i}

where we use the fact that
K\delta^{i}=T
by definition.

By repeated application of Young’s Inequality, we can bound, for any
\ell
and any
j\geq\ell
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x^{j}(t))}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\ell}^{j-1}3{(\frac{3}{2})}^{i-\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\ell}^{j-1}3{(\frac{3}{2})}^{i-\ell}\cdot 2^{-i}s_{i}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 12\cdot 2^{-\ell}\cdot s_{\ell}

Since the above holds for any
j
, we can take the limit of
j\to\infty
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x(t))}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle 12\cdot 2^{-\ell}\cdot s_{\ell}

where we use the fact that
\mathrm{d}{(x^{j}_{2^{j}},x(T))}
converges almost surely to
0
, from Lemma
5
.
∎

A.3
Discretization Error of Euler Murayama

Given the results of the previous section, we are now ready to prove Lemma
7
, which is informally stated as Lemma
1
in the Section
4
. The proof of Lemma
7
works by summing, for all
i
, the distance between
x^{i}(t)
and
x^{i+1}(t)
(which is bounded in Lemma
4
). Extra care must be taken to ensure that iterates do not stray too far from the initial error.

The crucial analysis corresponding to Step 1 (synchronous coupling) and Step 2 (rolling without slipping) discussed in the proof sketch of Lemma
1
in Section
4
can be found in the proof of Lemma
4
in Section
A.2
above.

Lemma 7
.

Let
M
satisfy Assumption
4
. Let
\beta(\cdot)
be a vector field satisfying Assumption
3
. Consider arbitrary
x(0)\in M
. Let
L_{1}
be any constant such that
L_{1}\geq{\left\|\beta(x(0))\right\|}
and let
T
be a step-size satisfying
T\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}},\frac{1}{16L_{R}d},\frac{1}{16\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{1}}\right\}
.

Let
x(t)
denote the solution to (
1.2
), initialized at
x(0)
. Let
x^{0}(t):=\Exp_{x(0)}{(t\beta(x(0))+\sqrt{t}\zeta)}
, where
\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}_{x(0)}(0,I)
. Then there exists a coupling between
x^{0}(T)
and
x(T)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{0}(T),x(T))}^{2}}\right]\leq 2^{20}{(T^{4}L_{1}^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+T^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+T^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}/{L_{1}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})}

Proof of Lemma
7
.

Let
E
be an orthonormal basis of
T_{x(0)}M
. Following the definition of
\overline{\Phi}
in (
A.3
), we verify that
x^{0}(T)=\overline{\Phi}(T;x(0),E,\beta,\mathbf{B},0)
, where
\mathbf{B}
is some Brownian motion, and equality is in the sense of distribution. On the other hand, by Lemma
2
,
x(t)=\Phi(t;x(0),E,\beta,\mathbf{B})
, where
\Phi
is the limit of
\overline{\Phi}
as defined in (
A.4
).

Step 1: Bounding the distance between
x^{i}(T)
and
x^{i+1}(T)

Let us consider some fixed
i
. Let
\delta^{i}=T/2^{i}
denote the stepsize, and let
K:=2^{i}
so that
T=K\delta^{i}
. Let
x^{i}(t)=\overline{\Phi}(T;x(0),E,\beta,\mathbf{B},i)
be as defined in (
A.3
). Recall that
x^{i}(t)
is by definition the linear interpolation of
x^{i}_{k}
(defined in (
4.1
) or equivalently (
A.2
)), which are marginally Euler-Murayama sequences with stepsize
\delta^{i}
.

Our goal is to bound
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),x^{i+1}(T))}^{2}}\right]
. Lemma
4
almost gives us what we want; the problem is that Lemma
4
assumes that for all
x\in M
,
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}
, but we do not make that assumption in this lemma. In order to get around this issue,
we use an argument based on truncating
\beta
at larger and larger norms
.

Let us define
L_{0}:={\left\|\beta(x(0))\right\|}
and

\displaystyle\beta^{j}(y):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\beta(y)&\text{for }{\left\|\beta(y)\right\|}\leq L_{1}2^{j/2+1}\\
\beta(y)\cdot\frac{L_{1}2^{j/2+1}}{{\left\|\beta(y)\right\|}}&\text{for }{\left\|\beta(y)\right\|}>L_{1}2^{j/2+1}\end{array}\right.,

(A.13)

i.e.
\beta^{j}
is the truncated version of
\beta
, so that the norm of
\beta^{j}
is globally upper bounded by
L_{1}2^{j/2+1}
.

Given this definition of
\beta^{j}
, we now define, for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
\tilde{x}^{i,j}(t):=\overline{\Phi}(t;x(0),E,\beta^{j},\mathbf{B},i)
, which is the (interpolated) Euler-Murayama discretization from (
A.3
), with step-size
\delta^{i}
, and drift
\beta^{j}
. In other words,
x^{i}(t)
and
\tilde{x}^{i,j}(t)
are both Euler Murayama discretizations with stepsize
\delta^{i}
, but the former has drift
\beta
whereas the latter has drift
\beta^{j}
. We also let
\tilde{x}^{\cdot,j}(t):={\Phi}(t;x(0),E,\beta^{i},\mathbf{B})
denote the limit, as
i\to\infty
, of
\tilde{x}^{i,j}(t)
(see definition in (
A.4
)).

By Young’s Inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle 8\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t))}^{2}+8\mathrm{d}{(x^{i+1}(t),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t))}^{2}+8\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t))}^{2}

(A.14)

Before proceeding, we briefly explain the intuition behind the decomposition in (
A.14
). Notice that we let
j=i
in
\tilde{x}^{i,i}
, i.e. as
i
increases, two this happen:
\delta^{i}
becomes smaller, and
\beta^{i}
becomes truncated at a larger norm (see (
A.13
)), and is thus closer to the true un-truncated
\beta
.

The first and second term on the right hand side of (
A.14
) correspond to error due to truncating
\beta
to
\beta^{i}
. These two terms would equal
0
if
\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t))}\leq\frac{L_{1}2^{i}/2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}
, as that implies (via Assumption
3
) that
\sup_{t}{\left\|\beta(x^{i}(t))\right\|}\leq L_{1}2^{i/2+1}
(so along the entire path,
\beta
was never large enough to require truncation). As
i
increases,
\frac{L_{1}2^{i}/2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}\to\infty
, and these two truncation errors are
0
with increasing probability.

The last term in (
A.14
) corresponds to error due to different discretization stepsize (
\delta^{i}
vs
\delta^{i+1}
).

Step 1.1: Bounding distance of truncated-drift sequences
\tilde{x}^{i,i}
and
\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}

We first bound the last term of (
A.14
). By the first claim of Lemma
4
, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(T))}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{10}e^{40T^{2}L_{R}L_{1}^{2}+2TL_{R}d+TL_{\beta}^{\prime}}T^{3}{(T^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{1}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+T{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{1}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-i}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{14-i}T^{3}{(T^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{1}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+T{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{1}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)},

where the first inequality uses the fact that
\delta^{i}=T/2^{i}\leq T
and that
\delta^{i}L_{1}2^{i+1}=2TL_{1}
, and second inequality is by our assumed upper bound on
T
. Here, we crucially use the fact that, the effect of
\delta^{i}
halving with
i
"cancels out" the effect of
{\left\|\beta^{i}(x)\right\|}
becoming larger with
i
.

Step 1.2: Bounding error due to truncation

We now bound the first two terms of (
A.14
). Once again recall from the definition in (
4.2
) and (
A.3
) that
x^{i}(t)
(resp
\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t)
) are linear interpolations of the discrete sequence
x^{i}_{k}
(resp
\tilde{x}^{i,i}_{k}
) as defined in (
4.1
). Under the event
\sup_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x(0))}\leq\frac{2^{i/2}L_{1}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}
, we verify that
x^{i}_{k}=\tilde{x}^{i,i}_{k}
for all
k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}
– this is because we can then bound, for all
k
,
{\left\|\beta(x^{i}_{k})\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x(0))}+L_{0}\leq 2^{i/2+1}L_{1}
, which in turn implies that for all
k
,
\beta(x^{i}_{k})
equals the truncated version
\beta^{i}(x^{i}_{k})
, which in turn implies that
\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T))}=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T))}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\sup_{k\in\left\{0..2^{i}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x(0))}>\frac{2^{i/2}L_{1}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T))}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{k\in\left\{0..2^{i}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x(0))}>\frac{2^{i/2}L_{1}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}})}}\cdot{(\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),x(0))}^{4}}\right]}+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T),x(0))}^{4}}\right]})}.

(A.15)

where the second line follows from Young’s inequality and Cauchy Schwarz.

From Lemma
16
, and our assumed bound on
T
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x(0))}>\frac{2^{i/2}L_{1}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}})}}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}}{L_{1}^{2}2^{i}}\exp{(1+8TL_{\beta}^{\prime}+2TL_{R}d+2T\delta^{i}L_{R}L_{0}^{2})}{(3Td+8T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{(Td+T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}}{L_{1}^{2}}\cdot{2^{4-i}}.

Also from Lemma
16
, for any
k
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x_{k},x(0))}^{4}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\exp{(2+16TL_{\beta}^{\prime}+4TL_{R}d+3T^{2}L_{R}L_{0}^{2})}{(T^{2}d^{2}+64T^{4}L_{0}^{4})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4{(T^{2}d^{2}+64T^{4}L_{0}^{4})}.

The same upper bound also applies to
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T),x(0))}^{4}}\right]
. Plugging into (
A.15
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T))}^{2}}\right]\leq 2^{12-i}\frac{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{(Td+T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}^{3}}{L_{1}^{2}}.

Note that the bound in Lemma
16
is strictly stronger for
x^{i+1}_{k}
compared to
x^{i}_{k}
. Thus by exactly identical steps, we can also upper bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{i+1}(T),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(T))}^{2}}\right]\leq 2^{12-i}\frac{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{(Td+T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}^{3}}{L_{1}^{2}}.

Plugging into (
A.14
), and applying Young’s Inequality, we finally have our bound between the Euler-Murayama sequences
x^{i}(T)
and
x^{i+1}(T)
(without truncation of
\beta
).

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),x^{i+1}(T))}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle 2^{14-i}T^{3}{(T^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{1}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+T{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{1}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}+2^{13-i}\frac{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{(Td+T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}^{3}}{L_{1}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{16-i}{(T^{4}L_{1}^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+T^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+T^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}/{L_{1}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})},

(A.16)

where we use the assumed upper bound on
T
in the lemma statement.

Step 2: Summing over
i

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we can summing (
A.16
) for
i\in\left\{0,1,2...\ell\right\}
to bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{0}(T),x^{\ell}(T))}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}3\cdot{(\frac{3}{2})}^{i}2^{16-i}{(T^{4}L_{1}^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+T^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+T^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}/{L_{1}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{20}{(T^{4}L_{1}^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+T^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+T^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}/{L_{1}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})}.

(A.17)

The first inequality uses triangle inequality and Young’s inequality recursively: for any
i
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),x^{\ell}(T))}^{2}\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i+1}(T),x^{\ell}(T))}^{2}+3\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),x^{i+1}(T))}^{2}.

Since (
A.17
) holds for all
\ell
, we take the limit of
\ell\to\infty
. By dominated convergence together with Lemma
5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{0}(T),x(T))}^{2}}\right]\leq 2^{20}{(T^{4}L_{1}^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+T^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+T^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}/{L_{1}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})}.

∎

A.4
Proof of Theorem
1

Below, we provide the full proof of Theorem
1
, which was sketched in Section
5
. The main results used are Lemma
3
(contraction of Lyapunov function under exact SDE) and Lemma
7
(bound on Euler Murayama discretization error).

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Step 0: Defining some Key Constants

In this step, we define a radius
r
, an event
A_{k}
based on
r
, and an upper bound on
\delta
.

\displaystyle c_{0}:=\log{(\frac{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}{m})}+\log{(\frac{{(1+L_{R})}d}{m})}+\log\mathcal{R}+\log{(K)},

\displaystyle r_{0}=32\sqrt{\frac{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{m}+\frac{L_{R}d^{2}}{m^{2}}+\frac{d}{m}\cdot c_{0}},

\displaystyle r=r_{0}+32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}\cdot\log{(1/\delta)}}.

(A.18)

Let
A_{k}
denote the event
\max_{i\leq k}\mathrm{d}{(x_{i},x^{*})}\leq r
. The value of
r
and
A_{k}
are chosen so that (
A.23
) holds in Step 1 below. Note that
r
depends on
\log(1/\delta)
on the last line.

We now define a suitable upperbound on the stepsize
\delta
. To do so, we first define the constants
c_{1},c_{5},c_{6},c_{7}
:

\displaystyle c_{1}:=\min\left\{\frac{1}{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}},\frac{1}{16L_{R}d},\frac{m}{64d\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}r_{0}},\frac{m}{64d\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}\sqrt{\log{(d\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}/m)}}}\right\},

(A.19)

\displaystyle c_{5}:=\frac{1}{64}\min\left\{\frac{m}{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}},\frac{d}{m}\right\},

\displaystyle c_{6}:=\frac{1}{64}\min\left\{\frac{m}{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\sqrt{L_{R}}r_{0}},\frac{d}{m\sqrt{L_{R}}r_{0}},\frac{d^{2}}{m^{2}r_{0}^{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle c_{7}:=\frac{1}{64}\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{m^{3}}{d{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}L_{R}^{2}\log{(\nicefrac{{{dL_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}L_{R}^{2}}}{{m^{3}}})}}},\sqrt{\frac{d}{mL_{R}\log{(\nicefrac{{mL_{R}}}{{d}})}}},{\frac{d}{m\log{(\nicefrac{{d}}{{m}})}}}\right\}.

(A.20)

For our proof, we require that
\delta
satisfies

\displaystyle\delta\leq\min\left\{c_{1},c_{5},c_{6},c_{7}\right\}

(A.21)

Thus
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
from the theorem statement is explicitly
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}=\frac{1}{\min\left\{c_{1},c_{5},c_{6},c_{7}\right\}}
. The motivation for this upper bound on
\delta
is so that
\delta
satisfies the conditions in Lemma
7
and Lemma
20
which are used in Step 1 and Step 3. We provide details in Step 4 below. Note that the upper bound on
\delta
depends only on
r_{0}
but not
r
, so the definition is not circular.

Step 1: Tail Bound:

We now show that with high probability, the discretization sequence
x_{k}
never steps outside the ball of radius
r
centered at
x^{*}
(this is exactly the event
A_{k}
that we defined in the previous step).

By Lemma
20
with
\sigma=0
and
\tilde{\beta}=\beta
, for any
(\delta,r)
satisfying

\delta\leq\min\left\{\frac{m}{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{(1+\sqrt{L_{R}}r)}},\frac{d}{m{(1+\sqrt{L_{R}}r)}},\frac{32{d^{2}}}{m^{2}r^{2}}\right\}
, we can bound the probability of
x_{k}
stepping outside the ball of radius
r
centered at
x^{*}
, for any
k\leq K
, as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({A_{k}^{c}})}=\mathbb{P}{({\max_{k\leq K}\mathrm{d}{(x_{k},x^{*})}\geq r})}\leq 32K\delta m\exp{(\frac{2{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{d}+\frac{64L_{R}{d}}{m}-\frac{mr^{2}}{256{d}})}.

(A.22)

Furthermore, we can bound the fourth-moment of the distance between
x_{K}
and
x^{*}
: by Lemma
17
with
\xi_{k}(x_{k})=\zeta_{k}
and
\sigma_{\xi}=2\sqrt{d}
, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x_{K},x^{*})}^{2}}\right]\leq\frac{2^{13}L_{R}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}d^{2}}{m^{6}}+\frac{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{m}+\frac{16d}{m}

Similarly, we can use Lemma
18
to bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x^{*})}^{2}}\right]\leq\frac{2^{13}L_{R}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}d^{2}}{m^{6}}+\frac{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{m}+\frac{32d}{m}.

Together, we can bound the expected distance between
y(K\delta)
and
x_{K}
under the low probability event
A_{K}^{c}
. By choosing
r
to be sufficiently large in (
A.18
), we make (
A.22
) sufficiently small. We then apply triangle inequality to bound
\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})}\leq\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x^{*})}+\mathrm{d}{(x_{K},x^{*})}
followed by Cauchy Schwarz, and verify that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{K}^{c}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})}}\right]\leq\sqrt{\delta}

(A.23)

Step 2: Continuous Time Contraction

Having established a bound in the distance under the event
A_{K}^{c}
, we now turn our attention to the high-probability event
A_{K}
.

Consider some fixed but arbitrary
k\leq K
. Let us define a continuous time SDE
\bar{x}^{k}(t)
, for
t\in[k\delta,(k+1)\delta]
, as the solution to the exact Langevin diffusion (
1.2
) initialized at
\bar{x}^{k}(k\delta):=x_{k}
.

The goal of this step is to bound
\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(y((k+1)\delta),\bar{x}^{k}((k+1)\delta))}}\right]
in terms of
\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(y(k\delta),\bar{x}^{k}(k\delta))}}\right]:=\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(y(k\delta),x_{k})}}\right]
. We apply Lemma
3
, which guarantees that there exists a coupling between the two exact SDE processes
y
and
\bar{x}^{k}
, such that
\mathbb{E}\left[{f(\mathrm{d}{(y(t),\bar{x}^{k}(t))})}\right]
contracts with rate
\alpha
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}f{(\mathrm{d}{(y((k+1)\delta),\bar{x}^{k}((k+1)\delta))})}}\right]\leq e^{-\alpha\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}f{(\mathrm{d}{(y(k\delta),x_{k})})}}\right],

(A.24)

where
f
is a Lyapunov function satisfying
f(r)\geq\frac{1}{2}\exp{(-{(q+L_{\Ric}/2)}\mathcal{R}^{2}/2)}r
and
{\left|f^{\prime}(r)\right|}\leq 1
, and
\alpha:=\min\left\{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{16},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{R}^{2}}\right\}\cdot\exp{(-\frac{1}{2}{(q+L_{\Ric}/2)}\mathcal{R}^{2})}
are as defined in Lemma
3
, and are consistent with the definition in our theorem statement.

Step 3: Euler Murayama Error

Next, we bound the distance between
x_{k+1}
and
\bar{x}^{k}((k+1)\delta)
. This represents the discretization error between a single Euler-Murayama step with stepsize
\delta
, and the exact Langevin diffusion over
\delta
time.

We will apply Lemma
7
with
L_{1}=L_{\beta}^{\prime}r
. We verify that under the event
A_{k}
and by Assumption
3
,
{\left\|\beta(x_{k})\right\|}
is indeed bounded by
L_{1}
. Thus by Lemma
7
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(x_{k+1},\bar{x}^{k}((k+1)\delta))}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2^{20}{(\delta^{4}L_{1}^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+\delta^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+\delta^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}/{L_{1}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2^{20}{(\delta^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}r^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+\delta^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+\delta^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+1/{\mathcal{R}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{O}{(\delta^{3/2})}

(A.25)

where
\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependency on
L_{\beta}^{\prime},d,L_{R},\mathcal{R},\log K,\log(1/\delta)
.

Combining (
A.24
) and (
A.25
) and using triangle inequality and the fact that
{\left|f^{\prime}\right|}\leq 1
, along with the fact that
A_{k+1}\subset A_{k}
, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k+1}\right\}}{f{(\mathrm{d}{(y((k+1)\delta),x_{k+1})})}}}\right]\leq e^{-\alpha\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}f{(\mathrm{d}{(y(k\delta),x_{k})})}}\right]+\tilde{O}{(\delta^{3/2})},

(A.26)

where
\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependency on
L_{\beta}^{\prime},d,L_{R},\mathcal{R},\log K,\log(1/\delta)
. This shows that, in one
\delta
-time step, the Lyapunov function of the distance contracts with rate
\alpha
, plus a discretization error of order
\delta^{3/2}
. Applying (
A.26
) recursively, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{K}\right\}}{f{(\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})})}}}\right]\leq e^{-\alpha K\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[{f{(\mathrm{d}{(y(0),x_{0})}^{2})}}\right]+\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\tilde{O}{(\delta^{1/2})}.

(A.27)

Combining (
A.23
) and (
A.27
), and using the fact that
f(r)\leq r
, gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{f{(\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})})}}}\right]\leq e^{-\alpha K\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[{f{(\mathrm{d}{(y(0),x_{0})}^{2})}}\right]+\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\tilde{O}{(\delta)}.

Using the fact that
\frac{1}{2}\exp{(-{(q+L_{\Ric}/2)}\mathcal{R}^{2}/2)}r\leq f(r)\leq r
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle e^{-\alpha K\delta+{(q+L_{Ric}/2)}\mathcal{R}^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y(0),x_{0})}}\right]+\frac{\exp{({(q+L_{Ric}/2)}\mathcal{R}^{2}/2)}}{\alpha}\cdot\tilde{O}{(\delta^{1/2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\alpha K\delta+{(q+L_{Ric}/2)}\mathcal{R}^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y(0),x_{0})}}\right]+\exp{({(q+L_{Ric}/2)}\mathcal{R}^{2})}\cdot\tilde{O}{(\delta^{1/2})},

where
\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependency on
L_{\beta}^{\prime},d,L_{R},\mathcal{R},\frac{1}{m-L_{Ric}/2},\log K,\log\frac{1}{\delta}
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1
.

Step 4: Verifying Conditions on
\delta

In the proof above, we applied Lemma
7
and Lemma
20
. Each of these requires certain bounds on
\delta
. In this step, we verify that the conditions on
\delta
for each of these lemmas is satisfied by (
A.21
).

Lemma
7
with
L_{1}=L_{\beta}^{\prime}r
requires

\displaystyle\delta\leq\frac{1}{16}\min\left\{\frac{1}{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}},\frac{1}{L_{R}d},\frac{1}{\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}r}\right\}

We verify that this follows from (
A.19
). We specifically verify that
\delta\leq\frac{1}{16\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}r}
is satisfied due to the last two terms in (
A.19
), and Lemma
37
.

Lemma
20
requires
requires
\delta\leq\min\left\{\frac{m}{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{(1+\sqrt{L_{R}}r)}},\frac{d+\sigma^{2}}{m{(1+\sqrt{L_{R}}r)}},\frac{32{(d^{2}+\sigma^{4})}}{m^{2}r^{2}}\right\}
. This is satisfied by (
A.20
) and Lemma
37
.

∎

A.5
Proof of Theorem
2

Below, we provide the full proof of Theorem
2
, which was sketched in Section
6
. The main results used are Lemma
8
(contraction of distance under Euler Murayama step) and Lemma
7
(bound on Euler Murayama discretization error).

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Step 0: Defining some Key Constants

In this step, we define a radius
r
, an event
A_{k}
based on
r
, and an upper bound on
\delta
.

\displaystyle c_{0}:=\log{(\frac{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}{m})}+\log{(\frac{{(1+L_{R})}{(d+\sigma^{2})}}{m})}+\log\mathcal{R}+\log{(K)},

\displaystyle r_{0}=32\sqrt{\frac{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{m}+\frac{L_{R}{(d^{2}+\sigma^{4})}}{m^{2}}+\frac{d}{m}\cdot c_{0}},

\displaystyle r=r_{0}+32\sqrt{\frac{d}{m}\cdot\log{(1/\delta)}}.

(A.28)

Let
A_{k}
denote the event
\max_{i\leq k}\max\left\{\mathrm{d}{(x_{i},x^{*})},\mathrm{d}{(y(i\delta),x^{*})}\right\}\leq r
. The value of
r
and
A_{k}
are chosen so that (
A.36
) holds in Step 1 below. Note that
r
depends on
\log(1/\delta)
on the last line.

We now define a suitable upperbound on the stepsize
\delta
. To do so, we first define the constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4},c_{5},c_{6},c_{7}
:

\displaystyle c_{1}:=\min\left\{\frac{1}{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}},\frac{1}{16L_{R}d},\frac{m}{64d\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}r_{0}},\frac{m}{64d\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}\sqrt{\log{(d\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}/m)}}}\right\},

(A.29)

\displaystyle c_{2}:=\min\left\{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{128{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{32L_{R}\sigma^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{13}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{\prime}d},\frac{(m-L_{Ric}/2)^{2}}{2^{24}{L_{R}^{\prime}}^{2}d^{3}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{17}d^{2}L_{R}^{2}}\right\},

(A.30)

\displaystyle c_{3}:=

\displaystyle\min\left\{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{32L_{R}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}r_{0}^{2}},\sqrt{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{14}{({L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{3}r_{0}^{3})}L_{R}^{\prime}}}\right\},

\displaystyle c_{4}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{128}\min\left\{\frac{m(m-L_{Ric}/2)}{32dL_{R}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\log{(\nicefrac{{dL_{R}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}}}{{m(m-L_{Ric}/2)}})}},\frac{(m-L_{Ric}/2^{1/2})m^{3/4}}{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{1/2}{L_{R}^{\prime}}^{1/2}d^{3/4}\log{(\nicefrac{{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{1/2}{L_{R}^{\prime}}^{1/2}d^{3/4}}}{{((m-L_{Ric}/2)^{1/2}m^{3/4})}})}}\right\},

(A.31)

\displaystyle c_{5}:=\frac{1}{64}\min\left\{\frac{m}{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}},\frac{d}{m}\right\},

\displaystyle c_{6}:=\frac{1}{64}\min\left\{\frac{m}{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\sqrt{L_{R}}r_{0}},\frac{d}{m\sqrt{L_{R}}r_{0}},\frac{d^{2}}{m^{2}r_{0}^{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle c_{7}:=\frac{1}{64}\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{m^{3}}{d{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}L_{R}^{2}\log{(\nicefrac{{{dL_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}L_{R}^{2}}}{{m^{3}}})}}},\sqrt{\frac{d}{mL_{R}\log{(\nicefrac{{mL_{R}}}{{d}})}}},{\frac{d}{m\log{(\nicefrac{{d}}{{m}})}}}\right\}.

(A.32)

For our proof, we require that
\delta
satisfies

\displaystyle\delta\leq\min\left\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4},c_{5},c_{6},c_{7}\right\}.

(A.33)

Thus
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
from the theorem statement is explicitly
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}=\frac{1}{\min\left\{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4},c_{5},c_{6}\right\}}
. The motivation for this upper bound on
\delta
is so that
\delta
satisfies the conditions in Lemma
7
, Lemma
8
and Lemma
20
which are used in Steps 1-3. We provide details in Step 4 below. Note that the upper bound on
\delta
depends only on
r_{0}
but not
r
, so the definition is not circular.

Step 1: Tail Bound:

We now show that with high probability, the discretization sequence
x_{k}
and the exact SDE
y(t)
never step outside the ball of radius
r
centered at
x^{*}
(this is exactly the event
A_{k}
that we defined in the previous step).

By Lemma
20
, for any
(\delta,r)
satisfying
\delta\leq\min\left\{\frac{m}{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{(1+\sqrt{L_{R}}r)}},\frac{d+\sigma^{2}}{m{(1+\sqrt{L_{R}}r)}},\frac{32{(d^{2}+\sigma^{4})}}{m^{2}r^{2}}\right\}
, we can bound the probability of
x_{k}
stepping outside the ball of radius
r
centered at
x^{*}
, for any
k\leq K
, as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\max_{k\leq K}\mathrm{d}{(x_{k},x^{*})}\geq r})}\leq 32K\delta m\exp{(\frac{2{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{d+\sigma^{2}}+\frac{64L_{R}{(d+\sigma^{2})}}{m}-\frac{mr^{2}}{256{(d+\sigma^{2})}})}.

(A.34)

On the other hand, by Lemma
2
, for
T=K\delta
, there is a family of discrete sequences,
y^{i}_{k}
, corresponding to (
1.2
) with stepsize
\delta^{i}=T/2^{i}
, whose linear interpolation
y^{i}(t)
converges to
y(t)
uniformly almost surely. By Lemma
20
with
\tilde{\beta}=\beta
, and
\sigma=0
, and stepsize
\delta^{i}
, and iteration number
K^{i}:=2^{i}
(so that
T=K^{i}\delta^{i}
), for
i
sufficiently large, we can bound
\mathbb{P}{({\max_{k\leq K^{i}}\mathrm{d}{(y^{i}_{k},x^{*})}\geq r})}\leq 32Tm\exp{(\frac{2{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{d}+\frac{64L_{R}d}{m}-\frac{mr^{2}}{256d})}
. Taking the limit of
i\to\infty
, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\max_{k\leq K}\mathrm{d}{(y(k\delta),x^{*})}\geq r})}\leq 32Tm\exp{(\frac{2{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{d}+\frac{32L_{R}d}{m}-\frac{mr^{2}}{256d})}.

(A.35)

Combining (
A.34
) and (
A.35
), we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}^{c}\right\}}}\right]=\mathbb{P}{({A_{k}^{c}})}\leq 64K\delta m\exp{(\frac{2{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{2}}{d}+\frac{64L_{R}{(d+\sigma^{2})}}{m}-\frac{mr^{2}}{256d})}.

Furthermore, we can bound the fourth-moment of the distance between
x_{K}
and
x^{*}
: by Lemma
17
with
\xi_{k}(x_{k})=\sqrt{\delta}{(\tilde{\beta}_{k}(x_{k})-\beta(x_{k}))}+\zeta_{k}
and
\sigma_{\xi}=2{(\sigma+\sqrt{d})}
, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x_{K},x^{*})}^{4}}\right]\leq\frac{2^{26}L_{R}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{8}{(\sigma^{8}+d^{4})}}{m^{12}}+\frac{128{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{4}}{m^{2}}+\frac{2^{10}{(d^{2}+\sigma^{4})}}{m^{2}}.

Similarly, we can use Lemma
18
to bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x^{*})}^{4}}\right]\leq\frac{2^{26}L_{R}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{8}d^{4}}{m^{12}}+\frac{128{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathcal{R}^{4}}{m^{2}}+\frac{512d^{2}}{m^{2}}.

Combining the above, we can bound the expected squared distance between
y(K\delta)
and
x_{K}
under the low probability event
A_{K}^{c}
. By choosing
r
to be sufficiently large in (
A.28
), we make (
A.35
) sufficiently small. We then apply Young’s inequality to bound
\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})}^{2}\leq 2\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x^{*})}^{2}+2\mathrm{d}{(x_{K},x^{*})}^{2}
followed by Cauchy Schwarz, to verify that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{K}^{c}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})}^{2}}\right]\leq\sqrt{\delta}.

(A.36)

Step 2: Discrete Contraction

Having established a bound in the distance under the event
A_{K}^{c}
, we now turn our attention to the high-probability event
A_{K}
.

Consider some fixed but arbitrary
k\leq K
. We define a useful intermediate variable, representing a single Euler-Murayama step initialized at
y(k\delta)
(recall that
y(t)
corresponds to the exact SDE):

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{k+1}:=\Exp_{y(k\delta)}{(\delta\beta(y(k\delta))+\sqrt{\delta}\bar{\zeta}_{k})}.

where
\bar{\zeta}_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}_{y(k\delta)}(0,I)
. Note that
\bar{y}_{k+1}
evolves according to the Euler-Murayama step with
exact drift

\beta(y(k\delta))
, whereas
x_{k+1}
evolves according to the Euler-Murayama step with
stochastic drift

\tilde{\beta}_{k}(x_{k})
.

Under the strong-convexity-like condition due to Assumption
2
with
\mathcal{R}=0
, we can show that a single discrete Euler Murayama step leads to contraction in distance between
\bar{y}_{k+1}
and
x_{k+1}
. From Lemma
8
, there exists a coupling such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(\bar{y}_{k+1},x_{k+1})}^{2}}\right]\leq{(1-{\delta(m-L_{Ric}/2)})}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(y(k\delta),x_{k})}^{2}}\right]+16\delta^{2}\sigma^{2}.

(A.37)

Step 3: Euler Murayama Error

Next, we bound the distance between
\bar{y}_{k+1}
and
y((k+1)\delta)
. This represents the discretization error between a single Euler-Murayama step with stepsize
\delta
, and the exact Langevin diffusion over
\delta
time.

We will apply Lemma
7
with
L_{1}=L_{\beta}^{\prime}r
. We verify that under the event
A_{k}
,
{\left\|\beta(y(k\delta))\right\|}
is indeed bounded by
L_{1}
. Thus by Lemma
7
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(y((k+1)\delta),\bar{y}_{k+1})}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle 2^{20}{(\delta^{4}L_{1}^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+\delta^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+\delta^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}/{L_{1}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{20}{(\delta^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}r^{4}{(1+L_{R})}+\delta^{4}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{4}+\delta^{3}{(d^{3}{(L_{R}+1/{\mathcal{R}^{2}})}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{O}{(\delta^{3})}

(A.38)

where
\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependency on
L_{\beta}^{\prime},d,L_{R},\mathcal{R},\log K,\log(1/\delta)
. This shows that, in one
\delta
-time step, the distance contracts with rate
m-L_{Ric}/2
, plus a discretization error of order
\delta^{2}
.

Combining (
A.37
) and (
A.38
) and using Young’s inequality inequality, together with the fact that
A_{k+1}\subset A_{k}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k+1}\right\}}{\mathrm{d}{(y((k+1)\delta),x_{k+1})}}^{2}}\right]\leq e^{-\delta{(m-L_{Ric}/2)}}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{k}\right\}}{\mathrm{d}{(y(k\delta),x_{k})}}^{2}}\right]+\tilde{O}{(\delta^{2})},

where
\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependency on
L_{\beta}^{\prime},d,L_{R},\mathcal{R},\sigma,\log K,\log(1/\delta)
.

Applying the above recursively, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{K}\right\}}{\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})}}^{2}}\right]\leq e^{-K\delta{(m-L_{Ric}/2)}}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathrm{d}{(y_{0},x_{0})}}^{2}}\right]+\frac{1}{m-L_{Ric}/2}\cdot\tilde{O}{(\delta)}.

(A.39)

Combining (
A.36
) with (
A.39
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),x_{K})}}^{2}}\right]\leq e^{-K\delta{(m-L_{Ric}/2)}}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\mathrm{d}{(y_{0},x_{0})}}^{2}}\right]+\frac{1}{m-L_{Ric}/2}\cdot\tilde{O}{(\delta)},

where
\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependency on
L_{\beta}^{\prime},d,L_{R},\mathcal{R},\sigma,\frac{1}{m-L_{Ric}/2},\log K,\log\frac{1}{\delta}
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
2
.

Step 4: Verifying Conditions on
\delta

In the proof above, we applied Lemma
7
, Lemma
8
and Lemma
20
. Each of these requires certain bounds on
\delta
. In this step, we verify that the conditions on
\delta
for each of these lemmas is satisfied by (
A.33
).

Lemma
7
with
L_{1}=L_{\beta}^{\prime}r
requires

\displaystyle\delta\leq\frac{1}{16}\min\left\{\frac{1}{{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}},\frac{1}{L_{R}d},\frac{1}{\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}r}\right\}.

We verify that this follows from (
A.29
). We specifically verify that
\delta\leq\frac{1}{16\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}r}
is satisfied due to the last two terms in (
A.29
), and Lemma
37
.

Lemma
8
with
L_{1}=rL_{\beta}^{\prime}
requires

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min\left\{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{128{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{32L_{R}\sigma^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{13}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{\prime}d},\frac{(m-L_{Ric}/2)^{2}}{2^{24}{L_{R}^{\prime}}^{2}d^{3}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{17}d^{2}L_{R}^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{32L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}},\sqrt{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{14}{(L_{\beta}^{3}+\sigma^{3})}L_{R}^{\prime}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min\left\{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{128{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{32L_{R}\sigma^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{13}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{\prime}d},\frac{(m-L_{Ric}/2)^{2}}{2^{24}{L_{R}^{\prime}}^{2}d^{3}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{17}d^{2}L_{R}^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{32L_{R}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}r^{2}},\sqrt{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{14}{({L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{3}r^{3}+\sigma^{3})}L_{R}^{\prime}}}\right\}

(A.40)

The first 5 bounds in (
A.40
) follow from (
A.30
). The last two bounds in (
A.40
) follow from (
A.31
) and Lemma
37
.

Lemma
20
requires
requires
\delta\leq\min\left\{\frac{m}{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{(1+\sqrt{L_{R}}r)}},\frac{d+\sigma^{2}}{m{(1+\sqrt{L_{R}}r)}},\frac{32{(d^{2}+\sigma^{4})}}{m^{2}r^{2}}\right\}
. This is satisfied by (
A.32
) and Lemma
37
.

∎

The following lemma shows that, for any two initial points
x
and
y
, if
x
undergoes an
exact
Euler Murayama step with drift
\beta
, and
y
undergooes a
stochastic
Euler Murayama step with drift
\tilde{\beta}
, then their expected squared distance contracts, with rate
m-L_{Ric}/2
, plus an additional error of
\delta^{2}\sigma^{2}
, where
\sigma={\left\|\tilde{\beta}-\beta\right\|}
. This lemma is somewhat analogous to Lemma
3
which shows contraction under the exact SDE, though Lemma
8
also requires a fair amount of additional discretization analysis.

The key result used in the proof of Lemma
8
is Lemma
29
.

Lemma 8
.

Let
M
satisfy Assumption
1
, Assumption
4
. Assume in addition that there exists a constant
L_{R}^{\prime}
such that for all
x\in M
,
u,v,w,z,a\in T_{x}M
,
\bm{\left\langle}(\nabla_{a}R)(u,v)w,z\bm{}\leq L_{R}^{\prime}{\left\|u\right\|}{\left\|v\right\|}{\left\|w\right\|}{\left\|z\right\|}{\left\|a\right\|}
. Let
\beta
be a deterministic vector field satisfying Assumption
3
and Assumption
2
, with
\mathcal{R}=0
. Let
x,y\in M
be arbitrary. Let
\tilde{\beta}
be a random vector field such that
\mathbb{E}\left[{\tilde{\beta}}\right]=\beta
. Assume that there exists
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
{\left\|\tilde{\beta}(y)-\beta(y)\right\|}\leq\sigma
. Let
L_{\beta}:=\max\left\{{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|},{\left\|\beta(y)\right\|}\right\}
. Let
x^{\prime}=\Exp_{x}{(\delta\beta(x)+\sqrt{\delta}\zeta)}
. Let
y^{\prime}=\Exp_{y}{(\delta\tilde{\beta}(y)+\sqrt{\delta}\tilde{\zeta})}
, where
\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}_{x}(0,I)
and
\tilde{\zeta}\sim\mathcal{N}_{y}(0,I)
.

Assume that

\displaystyle\delta\leq\min\left\{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{128{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{32L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{32L_{R}\sigma^{2}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{13}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{\prime}d},\frac{(m-L_{Ric}/2)^{2}}{2^{24}{L_{R}^{\prime}}^{2}d^{3}},\sqrt{\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{14}{(L_{\beta}^{3}+\sigma^{3})}L_{R}^{\prime}}},\frac{m-L_{Ric}/2}{2^{17}d^{2}L_{R}^{2}}\right\}.

Then there is a coupling (synchronous coupling) between
\zeta
and
\tilde{\zeta}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}^{2}}\right]\leq{(1-{\delta(m-L_{Ric}/2)})}\mathrm{d}{(x,y)}^{2}+16\delta^{2}\sigma^{2}

Note:
elsewhere in this paper, we have used
L_{\beta}
do denote a Lipschitz constant for
\beta
; the use of
L_{\beta}
in Lemma
8
is different (but related).

Proof.

Let
\gamma(s):[0,1]\to M
be a minimizing geodesic between
x
and
y
with
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=y
, such that
\bm{\langle}\Gamma_{y}^{x}{\beta(y)-\beta(x)},\gamma^{\prime}(0)\bm{\rangle}\leq-m\mathrm{d}{(x,y)}^{2}
. (Assumption
2
guarantees the existence of such a
\gamma
.)

Step 1: Synchronous Coupling of
\zeta
and
\tilde{\zeta}

We will now define a coupling between
\zeta
and
\tilde{\zeta}
. Let
E
be an orthonormal basis at
T_{x}M
, and let
F
be the parallel transport of
E
along
\gamma
, i.e.
F
is an orthonormal basis for
T_{y}M
. Let
{\bm{\zeta}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
be a standard Gaussian random variable in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and define
\zeta:={\bm{\zeta}}\circ E
, and it follows by definition that
\zeta
so defined has distribution
\mathcal{N}_{x}(0,I)
. Let
\tilde{\zeta}:=\tilde{{\bm{\zeta}}}\circ F
, it follows by definition that
\tilde{\zeta}
has distribution
\mathcal{N}_{y}(0,I)
.

Step 2: Applying Lemma
29
and Simplifications

We will apply Lemma
29
with
u=\delta\beta(x)+\sqrt{\delta}\zeta
and
v=\delta\tilde{\beta}(y)+\sqrt{\delta}\tilde{\zeta}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x}(u),\Exp_{y}(v))}^{2}-\mathrm{d}{(x,y)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{2\bm{\left\langle}\gamma^{\prime}(0),v(0)-u(0)\bm{}}_{\hbox to9.22pt{\vbox to9.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60942pt\lower-4.60942pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.1 0 C 6.1 3.37 3.37 6.1 0 6.1 C -3.37 6.1 -6.1 3.37 -6.1 0 C -6.1 -3.37 -3.37 -6.1 0 -6.1 C 3.37 -6.1 6.1 -3.37 6.1 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.99306pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}+\underbrace{{\left\|v(0)-u(0)\right\|}^{2}}_{\hbox to9.22pt{\vbox to9.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60942pt\lower-4.60942pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.1 0 C 6.1 3.37 3.37 6.1 0 6.1 C -3.37 6.1 -6.1 3.37 -6.1 0 C -6.1 -3.37 -3.37 -6.1 0 -6.1 C 3.37 -6.1 6.1 -3.37 6.1 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.99306pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{-\int_{0}^{1}\bm{\left\langle}R{(\gamma^{\prime}(s),(1-s)u(s)+sv(s))}(1-s)u(s)+sv(s),\gamma^{\prime}(s)\bm{}ds}_{\hbox to9.22pt{\vbox to9.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60942pt\lower-4.60942pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
